# Threat model (STRIDE)

Each prompt fills in the section for the components it owns, and keeps it current. A human signs off before go-live (S24).

For each component, record:

- **Assets.**
- **Trust boundaries.**
- **STRIDE threats:** Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information disclosure, Denial of service and Elevation of privilege. Give each threat its mitigation, with the S# it maps to.
- **Residual risks.**

## Components

- Agent (02)
- Hub identity and audit (03a)
- Hub platform: gateway, routing, events, Git, webhooks (03b)
- Engine (04)
- Registry and read API (05)
- Write paths and token vault (06)
- MCP server (07)
- Web app (08)
- Deployment and supply chain (09)
- Docs search (14)
- NFS VM sentinel (17)

## Contracts, ports, migrations, fixtures and fuzz skeleton (01)

Owner: prompt 01. This section covers what 01 creates and everything else builds on: the database roles and the audit table protections (`db/migrations/0001_init.sql`, `deploy/dev/init.sql`), the `Secret<T>` type, the typed validators (`NfsPath`, `RepoPath`, `CompareRef`, `GitRef`, `TenantId`, `PathMappingRule`), the proto and OpenAPI contracts, the synthetic fixture generator and the fuzz skeleton. Behaviour behind the ports belongs to the prompts that implement them and is covered in their sections.

### Assets

- The audit chain (`audit_events`, `audit_checkpoints`): the evidence of who changed what (S9).
- Secrets in memory: GitHub tokens, session ids, join tokens, webhook URLs, keys (S7, S8, S21).
- The NFS tree, which only typed paths may address (S11, S17).
- The two database roles, `lanekeeper_migrator` (owns the schema, runs DDL) and `lanekeeper_app` (the hub's runtime identity).
- The contract files themselves, which every other prompt trusts.

### Trust boundaries

- Hub process to PostgreSQL: the hub connects as `lanekeeper_app` and never as the migrator or a superuser.
- Network or API input to the typed validators: the first parse of every path, compare reference, tenant id and swimlane id.
- Agent and sentinel to hub over gRPC: message size limits and the join request shape live in `proto/agent.proto` and `crates/proto`.
- Developer machine and CI to the repository: fixtures are generated, never copied from a real environment.

### STRIDE

| | Threat | Mitigation | S# |
|---|---|---|---|
| S | An agent presents itself as a sentinel, or the reverse, to obtain the other identity. | `JoinRequest` carries `PeerKind`; the hub issues a certificate SAN that includes the kind and authorises RPCs by kind. `UNSPECIFIED` is rejected. No agent-facing join-token endpoint exists in `api/openapi.yaml` (`no_agent_join_token_endpoint`). | S5, S25 |
| S | A user acts as someone else through a forged or replayed browser session. | Every non-GET operation in the OpenAPI contract requires CSRF and `Idempotency-Key`; every operation declares `x-required-role`; the route-guard harness (`ports::conformance::route_guard`) fails any route without a matching guard. Implemented by 03a. | S3, S4 |
| S | The agent is made to call the unauthenticated config-server on its own, or at a host a message names. | The only callers are the dispatcher's two arms, `NotifyConfigServer` and `FetchServed`, so every call answers a hub command that the hub has authorised (Operator) and audited. The client is built once, from `LK_CONFIG_SERVER_URL`, and dials that host and port for every request; no message carries a host, a scheme or a port, and a name can only fill a path segment. Redirects are not followed. Without the variable both commands are answered `UNSUPPORTED`. | `notify_only_on_hub_command`, `config_server_calls_only_from_the_dispatcher`, `config_server_scan_rejects_planted_violations`, `fetch_served_url_cannot_escape`, `without_a_configured_url_the_config_server_commands_are_unsupported` | S17 |
| T | The application role, or code running as it, edits or deletes audit rows. | `lanekeeper_app` holds only INSERT and SELECT on `audit_events` and `audit_checkpoints` (explicit per-table grants, no default privileges). A trigger refuses UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE for every role, the owner and superusers included, and is `ENABLE ALWAYS`, so `session_replication_role = replica` does not bypass it. Tests: `app_cannot_update_audit_events`, `app_cannot_delete_audit_events`, `app_cannot_truncate_audit_events`, `trigger_blocks_update_and_delete_even_for_owner`, `app_has_only_insert_select_on_audit_events`. | S9 |
| T | The audit chain is forked or a row is forged with a wrong hash. | `UNIQUE(prev_hash)` stops a fork; a check constraint requires `hash = sha256(prev_hash \|\| event_json)`; the canonical JSON that was hashed is stored. Tests: `audit_prev_hash_cannot_fork`. | S9 |
| T | The runtime role changes the schema, for example to add a trigger or grant itself rights. | `lanekeeper_app` owns nothing and has no DDL right (`app_cannot_run_ddl`). Roles are created outside the migration (`deploy/dev/init.sql`, CloudNativePG managed roles) and the migration refuses to run as any other role. Every table needs a recorded grant decision (`every_table_has_a_grant_decision`), so a new table cannot silently inherit broad rights. | S9, S21 |
| T | A path escapes the NFS root (`..`, absolute path, backslash, NUL, control character, over-long path). | `NfsPath` is the only path type that reaches the agent or sentinel. It rejects every traversal form, NUL and control characters, backslashes and absolute paths, normalises `//` and `.`, and bounds the length (1,024 bytes; 255 per component). Fuzz target `nfs_path` asserts the invariants on arbitrary input, and its seed corpus replays on stable in `corpus_replay.rs`. The agent's cap-std root (02) is the second layer. | S11, S17, S22 |
| T | A tenant suffix is split wrongly, so a write lands on the wrong file. | `PathMappingRule::reverse` strips only an exact `-<tenant>` for a known tenant (the longest wins) and never splits on the first hyphen. Fuzz target `path_mapping_reverse` asserts that forward mapping of the reverse result gives the original path. | S11, S22 |
| T | A compare reference smuggles a path, option or ref syntax (`..`, `@{`, a leading `-`) into a Git call. | `CompareRef` and `GitRef` follow `git check-ref-format` rules, are bounded, and are rejected rather than repaired. Fuzz target `compare_ref` asserts a display and parse round trip. | S11, S22 |
| T | A tampered dependency or lockfile changes behaviour. | Exact version pins at least 14 days old, a committed `Cargo.lock`, `cargo deny` and `cargo audit` in `just verify`, and a separate committed lockfile for the fuzz crate, checked by `just fuzz-smoke`. | S19 |
| T | A file name or path in a command changes the request to the config-server: another segment, a query, a second form field, a header, another endpoint. | The request target is built from validated pieces (`AppName`, `TenantId`, `ChannelName`, `NfsPath`) and every byte that is not unreserved is percent-encoded, the file component by component; the refresh form encodes each path as one `path=` value. Header values come from constants. | `fetch_served_url_cannot_escape`, `notify_form_encodes_what_would_break_a_form`, `a_target_never_changes_shape`, `a_form_body_has_one_field_per_path_and_nothing_else`, `agent_hub_message` | S11, S17 |
| R | A state change leaves no trace, or the trace is rewritten afterwards. | Audit events are written in the same transaction as the change (`AuditLog::record(&mut tx, ..)`). The chain is append-only and hash-linked as above; 03a adds hourly KMS-signed checkpoints in a locked-retention bucket, so a rewrite is detectable. | S9 |
| I | A secret reaches a log, an error, an audit event or a response. | `Secret<T>` prints `[redacted]` in `Debug`, has no `Display`, no `Serialize` and no `Clone`, compares only in constant time (`ct_eq`) and is zeroized on drop; `Deserialize` exists for `Secret<String>` only, for loading configuration. Tests: `debug_is_redacted`, `zeroize_on_drop`, and static assertions that `Secret` is not `Serialize`, `Display`, `Clone` or `PartialEq` (`crates/domain/tests/secret.rs`). The generated `Debug` of `JoinRequest` is replaced by a redacting one (`join_tokens_never_reach_debug_output`). Validator and port error enums never carry the rejected input (`errors_do_not_leak_inputs`). | S7, S21 |
| I | Real tenant configuration leaks through test data. | `cargo xtask gen-fixtures` builds everything from a seed with invented names and content; output goes under `target/fixtures/`, which is git-ignored; nothing real is read or committed. Two runs with the same seed give identical output, so the data is reproducible and reviewable. | AGENTS rule 12 |
| I | The application role reads more than it needs. | Per-table grants: append-only, write-once and mutable tables are distinct decisions; no TRUNCATE, REFERENCES or TRIGGER is ever granted; `_sqlx_migrations` is not readable by `lanekeeper_app`. | S9, S21 |
| I | `FetchServed` returns a keystore, a key or other denied material that the config-server would serve, or an error that names it. | The request is checked against the shared deny list (D79) before anything is sent: the file as named, under the application's folder, inside the channel's folder, and the tenant's copy (`name-<tenant>.ext`) that the server prefers. A denied request is answered `DENIED` with no path in the answer or the log, costs no rate-limit place and reaches the config-server not at all. Only a `2xx` carries a body; the text of any other answer is dropped. | `fetch_served_never_returns_denied_bytes_or_names`, `fetch_served_refuses_denied_names_without_a_request`, `fetch_served_follows_the_same_deny_list_as_file_reads`, `fetch_served_checks_the_tenant_copy_the_server_would_pick`, `fetch_served_error_status_comes_back_without_the_servers_text`, `denied_requests_do_not_use_the_rate_limit` | S10, S17 |
| D | Oversized or unbounded input exhausts memory. | gRPC messages are capped at 4 MiB after decompression, a `ScanDelta` at 3 MiB and a fixed entry count (`proto::limits`); OpenAPI lists use keyset pagination with a `limit` of at most 500; MCP inputs and outputs are bounded (`docs/mcp-tools.md`, lint in `mcp_tools_doc.rs`); `TenantSet` is capped; every path and ref has a length limit. | S11, S14 |
| D | The sentinel partition maintenance blocks inserts or fills the disk. | A default partition means an insert never fails for want of a partition. `maintain_sentinel_partitions` is idempotent, serialised by an advisory lock and validates `keep_days` (90 to 3650; see the Elevation of privilege row). | S9 |
| D | The agent is used to flood or stall the config-server, or a hostile server stalls or fills the agent. | `FetchServed` is limited to five requests in any second for the whole agent (a sliding window; over it, `429` here and no call); a response over 2 MiB is refused whole; a refresh reads at most 64 KiB of answer. Every request has a 5-second timeout; a refresh is tried three times with full-jitter pauses of at most 0.2 s and 0.4 s, a read once. At most four commands run at once. A status outside 100 to 599 is an `IO` failure, not a message the hub would drop. | `fetch_served_rate_limit_5_per_s`, `the_limit_holds_in_every_one_second_window`, `the_limiter_is_bounded_whatever_the_load`, `fetch_served_caps_body_at_2mib`, `notify_timeout_5s_with_bounded_retries`, `fetch_served_connect_failure_and_timeout_answer_io`, `a_status_the_hub_would_reject_is_a_failure_not_an_answer`, `the_notify_answer_body_is_capped_and_never_used` | S17 |
| E | The one `SECURITY DEFINER` function is used to run arbitrary DDL. | `maintain_sentinel_partitions(integer)` is owned by the migrator with a pinned `search_path` (`pg_catalog, pg_temp`), schema-qualified names and format-quoted identifiers; it takes only an integer; `EXECUTE` is revoked from PUBLIC and granted to `lanekeeper_app` alone. It can only create and drop partitions named `sentinel_records_yYYYYmMM` of that one table. It refuses (SQLSTATE `22023`) any `keep_days` below 90 and above 3650. The floor of 90 is a constant in the function body, not a parameter, a setting or a table the app can write, so a compromised hub cannot use the function to prune a partition that still ends inside the 90-day life. The grants and the foreign key close the other paths to the same records: `lanekeeper_app` has only SELECT and INSERT on `sentinel_records` (no UPDATE, DELETE or TRUNCATE), and `sentinel_records.sentinel_id` is `ON DELETE RESTRICT`, so `DELETE FROM sentinels` is refused (SQLSTATE `23503`) while the sentinel has records instead of cascading into them. Tests: `sentinel_partition_function_is_the_only_ddl_path`, `sentinel_partition_function_refuses_short_retention`, `app_cannot_erase_sentinel_records_by_deleting_the_sentinel`, `no_cascading_foreign_key_touches_a_protected_table`. | S9, S21 |
| E | A proposal's author approves their own change. | A `BEFORE INSERT` trigger on `approvals` refuses an approval row whose approver is the proposal's author. This guards the insert path against a bug in the hub. It does not protect against a caller that holds UPDATE or DELETE on `approvals` or `proposals`: `lanekeeper_app` has both, so it can change `approver_oid` or `author_oid` after the insert, or delete a proposal, which cascades to its approvals. The approval rule is therefore also enforced in application code (prompt 06, S4/D37). The gap is accepted as a residual risk (below). | S4 |
| E | A route or MCP tool is added without a role. | Every OpenAPI operation needs a valid `x-required-role`; the route-guard harness and the MCP doc lint (role floor against OpenAPI, confirmation on write tools) fail otherwise. | S4, S14 |

### Residual risks

- **Owner-level database access.** The migrator owns the tables, so it can disable or drop the audit triggers. Mitigation is outside this prompt: the migrator credential is used only by the migration job, not by the hub, and the signed checkpoints (03a, S9) let a nightly job detect a rewrite. A rewrite after the latest checkpoint, at most one hour, is detectable only by the Cloud Logging copy.
- **Self-approval is guarded on insert only.** The `approvals_no_self_approval` trigger fires `BEFORE INSERT`. `lanekeeper_app` holds SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on `approvals` and `proposals`, so code running as that role (a hub bug or a compromised hub) can insert an approval by another user and then `UPDATE approvals SET approver_oid` to the author, `UPDATE proposals SET author_oid`, or `DELETE` a proposal (the cascade removes its approvals). Mitigation: approval enforcement (S4/D37) is also done in application code in prompt 06. Closing the gap (SELECT and INSERT only on `approvals`, a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger on `approvals`, an author-immutability trigger on `proposals`) is a possible hardening for prompt 06 or a later contract-change. Accepted by a human at the round 3 review of prompt 01 (reword only).
- **The definer function is a privileged surface.** It is small and bounded. Anything that can call it as `lanekeeper_app` can drop sentinel partitions, but only whole months that ended more than 90 days ago (the floor is fixed in the function), plus default-partition rows older than 90 days; the audit tables are not touched, and deleting a sentinel does not cascade into its records. A caller can therefore make expiry happen when it is due and no earlier. It cannot make records last longer or shorter than the hub's own schedule decides above the floor. Lowering the floor is a migration and a recorded decision, not a runtime setting. Two limits remain: a caller can run the function repeatedly, which takes an advisory lock and briefly locks the parent table (a denial-of-service nuisance, not a data loss), and an owner-level credential is outside this control (see the first bullet).
- **`Secret::expose` is an escape hatch.** The type stops accidental formatting and serialisation, not a caller that reads the plaintext and logs it. Reviewers grep for `expose()`, and the log-capture tests of later prompts cover the paths that matter.
- **Rejection of unusual file names.** `NfsPath` rejects backslashes and control characters, so a real file with such a name cannot be addressed. This is deliberate (plan Q13); it surfaces as a finding rather than a silent skip.
- **A base file whose name ends in a known tenant id** is reported as a tenant file (plan Q14). This is a limit of the naming scheme, documented in `mapping.rs`.
- **Fuzzing coverage is a skeleton.** Three targets run 30 seconds each per smoke run. Parsers added by later prompts (YAML, `.properties`, Markdown chunking, webhook payloads, proto decoding at the hub) must add their own targets (S22).
- **The development database passwords** in `deploy/dev/init.sql` are for local use only; production roles come from CloudNativePG managed roles (09).

## Agent (02)

Owner: prompt 02. This section covers `crates/agent` as built by tasks T1 to T12: configuration and the cap-std root, joining the hub and renewing the certificate, the gRPC transport, the Merkle tree and scans, the durable spool, quiescence and sync tagging, deny globs, the config-server calls, file operations, the Kubernetes watchers and restarts, operations (health, metrics, logs), and the pod contract (`crates/agent/Dockerfile`, `HARDENING.md`, `RBAC.md`). The hub's side of the same links (issuing certificates, authorising commands, auditing) is 03a and 03b; the NFS VM sentinel is 17. The config-server calls (T12) are the agent's only use of an endpoint with no authentication (Q37), so they get rows of their own below.

The agent reports facts and carries out commands. It decides nothing: the hub checks the user's role, writes the audit event and sends the command. So this section asks two questions of the agent: can an outsider use it, and can a command from the hub do more than its shape allows?

### Assets

- **The client private key and certificate** (ECDSA P-256; the certificate lives 24 hours). They prove which swimlane the agent is (S5). The key is made in memory and stored only in the certificate Secret.
- **Join credentials:** the Google ID token for the agent's service account, and the one-time join token of the fallback (single use, valid 24 hours). Either one gets a certificate.
- **The NFS export of one swimlane** (`LK_NFS_ROOT`): the files the config-server serves. Integrity matters most, because a wrong byte is served to a bank's services. The files hold no secrets by design (D18), but keystores and keys may sit among them (D79).
- **The stream to the hub**, which carries file reads, writes, deletes and restarts.
- **The spool volume** (`LK_SPOOL_DIR`, a small persistent volume of its own, outside the NFS export): a copy of every version the scanner saw and the hub has not yet acknowledged, up to 512 MiB or 100,000 entries. It holds file content at rest, so it is as sensitive as the export. Integrity matters too: a version in it is sent to the hub as if the agent had just seen it.
- **The agent's Kubernetes access:** its service-account token, the Roles in `RBAC.md` (get, list and watch on Deployments, Pods and Jobs, and patch on Deployments, in the configured namespaces; get and update on the certificate Secret and get on the join-token Secret, both by name).
- **Cluster facts the agent reports:** Deployment and pod names, labels, environment variable names, and the values of the names on the hub's allowlist (D88).
- **Logs and metrics:** paths, hashes and ids, which must stay free of contents and secrets (S10).
- **Availability and freshness:** the agent's tree is how the hub learns of an out-of-band change within 15 seconds (P1).

### Trust boundaries

- **Agent to hub** (gRPC over TLS 1.3, one pinned CA, mutual TLS on the stream, no client certificate on `Join`). The hub is trusted to authorise commands. The agent still checks the shape of everything it receives, and clamps the settings the hub sends.
- **Agent to the spool volume.** Written and read only by the agent. What it reads back after a crash is untrusted: a cut or damaged file, or one that someone with access to the volume changed. Each record is checked (length, checksum, metadata) and then decoded and validated like a message from anywhere before it is sent.
- **Agent to the NFS export.** The export is written by others too: the config-server's jobs, the dataload Job, people on the VM. Everything under the root is untrusted input: names, symlinks, sizes, special files.
- **Agent to the Kubernetes API server** (service-account token, namespaced Roles). Responses are untrusted text and are trimmed on ingest.
- **Agent to the node's metadata server** (`169.254.169.254`, plain HTTP, node-local), for the ID token.
- **Agent to the config-server pods** (port 8888, plain HTTP, no authentication, Q37). The agent calls it in two ways only (a refresh and a served-file read, `src/configserver.rs`), each in answer to one hub command, and only at the address in `LK_CONFIG_SERVER_URL`. The NetworkPolicy contract limits egress to these pods on port 8888.
- **Container to node and cluster:** non-root, read-only root file system, no capabilities, RuntimeDefault seccomp, no shell, egress limited to five destinations (`HARDENING.md`).
- **Kubelet and Prometheus to the health port** (`9090`, plain HTTP, no authentication, three read-only paths).
- **Build to deployment:** the image is built from `crates/agent/Dockerfile` on digest-pinned bases with `--locked`; crates are exact pins (S19).
- **Chart and operator to the agent:** the environment variables are trusted configuration, validated at start.

### STRIDE

A row's Proof cell names tests, benchmarks or fuzz targets that exist in `crates/agent`; `tests/threat_model.rs` fails if one does not. A control that is not built yet has a Proof cell that starts with `None`.

| | Threat | Mitigation | Proof | S# |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S | Something stands in for the hub (DNS, routing, a certificate for the hub's name from a public CA) to collect the agent's ID token or join token, or to send it commands. | The only trust anchors are the certificates in `LK_HUB_CA_FILE`; the system roots are never consulted. TLS 1.3 and nothing older, no session resumption. `Join` runs over the same verified TLS, so a credential is only ever sent to a hub the pinned CA vouches for. A hub that offers only TLS 1.2 is refused before a byte is sent. | `server_cert_from_other_ca_is_refused`, `a_hub_certificate_from_another_ca_is_refused`, `client_config_is_tls13_only`, `tls12_only_server_is_refused`, `a_tls12_only_hub_is_refused_before_anything_is_sent` | S5, S6 |
| S | The hub, or something posing as it, issues the agent an identity for another swimlane, another kind of peer or another key, and the agent then acts under it. | Before a certificate is stored or used, `ClientIdentity::verify` requires: our own public key, exactly one URI SAN, `spiffe://lanekeeper/swimlane/<id>` with `<id>` our own swimlane id (S5, decision A5), a lifetime of at most 25 hours, and not expired. The SAN form is written once, in `AGENT_SAN_PREFIX`. The hub side (03b) issues only after checking the ID token's service account against the swimlane's registered one, and checks that the SAN equals the swimlane in `Hello`. | `issued_cert_san_must_be_swimlane_form`, `rejects_issued_cert_with_wrong_key_san_or_lifetime`, `agent_san_defined_in_one_constant`, `agent_cert_chain` | S5 |
| S | A weaker credential is used where a stronger one exists: the join token replaces a Workload Identity token that is merely failing. | The join token is used only when the metadata server is unreachable and a token Secret is configured. A metadata server that answers with an error, or a hub that refuses the ID token, is never a reason to try the other way. The modes `workload-identity` and `token` each use one credential and never touch the other. | `join_token_fallback_only_without_workload_identity`, `join_mode_token_never_calls_metadata_server`, `join_mode_workload_identity_never_reads_the_join_token` | S5 |
| S | Another pod uses the agent's identity: it asks the metadata server for a token, or reads the certificate Secret. | The ID token is for the Google service account bound to the agent's Kubernetes service account, which Workload Identity enforces per service account (a cluster property, Q26). The Role for the two Secrets is bound to the agent's service account alone, and names the Secrets. The remaining exposure is in the residual risks (cluster admins, root on the node). | `rbac_secret_rules_are_named_and_minimal`, `rbac_manifest_has_no_secret_list` | S5, S17 |
| S | A forged, truncated or hostile message arrives on the stream. | Every hub message is validated by `proto::convert` before anything else sees it. An invalid one is dropped and counted; an invalid command with a readable request id is answered `DENIED` with no detail. Unknown message kinds are skipped, and the stream stays open. | `invalid_hub_message_is_dropped_stream_survives`, `hostile_command_is_answered_denied_before_io`, `agent_hub_message` | S11, S22 |
| T | A command's path escapes the NFS root (`..`, an absolute path, a backslash, NUL, a control character, an over-long name). | `NfsPath` is the only path type that reaches a file operation. Then every operation walks the path one component at a time through the cap-std `Dir`, which refuses an escape itself. The source is scanned: no `std::fs`, `tokio::fs` or `File::open` anywhere, and an ambient directory open only in `root.rs` (the NFS root), `tls.rs` (the CA file) and `spool/volume.rs` (the spool directory). | `hostile_command_is_answered_denied_before_io`, `traversal_corpus_never_escapes_root`, `all_file_access_via_cap_std`, `agent_path` | S11, S17, S22 |
| T | A symbolic link planted on the export by someone who can write there leads a read, write or delete to another file. | No symlink is ever followed, even one that stays inside the root. Each directory on the way and the file are `lstat`ed first and refused if they are links; the opened handle must have the same device and inode as the listed entry, so a directory swapped for a link in between is refused. The walker records a link as an entry of kind 2 with an all-zero hash. | `symlinked_file_refused`, `symlinked_dir_refused`, `symlinks_are_not_followed_and_are_reported`, `a_file_replaced_by_a_symlink_is_removed_and_skipped`, `agent_path` | S17 |
| T | A write is blind or lost: it overwrites a file that changed since the user looked, or a half-written file is served. | Every write carries the expected hash (or "must not exist"), compared in constant time with the hash on disk. A mismatch writes nothing and returns `CONFLICT` with the current hash. A write goes to a temporary file in the same directory, is fsynced, takes the old file's mode, and is renamed over the target, then the directory is fsynced; a crash leaves the old file. A create uses a hard link, which fails if the name has appeared. A delete also needs the hash. | `hash_mismatch_leaves_file_untouched`, `expected_absent_conflicts_when_file_exists`, `delete_requires_matching_hash`, `crash_before_rename_keeps_original`, `same_path_operations_are_serialised` | S11 |
| T | A write changes the bytes: line endings, a byte-order mark or the encoding are normalised. | Bytes are never interpreted: a write stores exactly the bytes it was given, and a scan hashes exactly the bytes on disk. | `crlf_and_bom_roundtrip_byte_exact`, `binary_roundtrip`, `bytes_are_never_normalised` | S11 |
| T | A command makes the agent create or touch what it should not: new directories, its own temporary names, oversized files. | A write never creates a parent directory (a new folder needs a config-server restart, C11). Names that start with `.lanekeeper-tmp-` or `.nfs` are refused. A file over 2 MiB is refused whatever limit the hub sends, and the hub's limit can only lower the cap. | `write_does_not_create_parent_directories`, `reserved_names_are_not_for_the_hub`, `write_over_2mib_refused`, `tunables_clamp_hub_values` | S11 |
| T | A change on the export is lost or misreported: a failed read looks like a deletion, an unmounted export looks like a mass deletion, or a change that restores the mtime hides. | A scan error never becomes a removal: a directory that cannot be listed keeps its previous subtree. An empty root after a non-empty tree is held for up to 30 seconds before it is accepted, so a mount that drops briefly does not look like a deletion of everything. A full rehash every 15 minutes finds changes the stat walk cannot. File bytes are re-hashed when they are read for sending, and the tree is patched with what was actually sent. | `scan_error_never_reports_removal`, `unmounted_root_is_not_mass_deletion`, `full_rehash_finds_a_change_with_restored_mtime` | S11, S17 |
| T | The agent restarts or changes workloads it should not. | `Restarter` refuses any namespace not in `LK_NAMESPACES` before it calls the API. The patch sets only the `restartedAt` annotation. The Roles are per namespace; there is no ClusterRole. | `restart_refused_outside_configured_namespaces`, `restart_patches_annotation`, `rbac_manifest_has_no_secret_list` | S17 |
| T | The image or a dependency is replaced. | Distroless base and builder both pinned by digest, one binary built with `--locked`, exact crate pins at least 14 days old, `cargo deny` and `cargo audit` over the main lockfile and the agent's fuzz lockfile. | `dockerfile_is_distroless_nonroot_pinned`, `dockerfile_lint_rejects_planted_violations` | S16, S19 |
| T | The spool's files are cut by a crash, damaged by the disk, or changed or planted by someone with access to the volume, and the agent sends a version that was never observed or loses the ones after it. | Every record has a length (checked against the largest wire message before anything is read or allocated) and a CRC-32; opening reads each segment once and trusts a record only if its checksum matches. A record that is read back is decoded and validated by the same conversion as a message from the network, and a record whose metadata disagrees with the index is refused. A damaged tail ends its segment, is cut off, and is reported to the hub as a gap; what follows the damage in that segment is lost, never guessed. The CRC finds accidents, not tampering: a record someone forged with a valid checksum is still only a message the hub validates and authorises. | `spool_corrupt_tail_skipped_and_reported`, `damage_in_the_middle_of_a_segment_costs_what_follows_it_and_no_more`, `spool_truncation_at_every_offset_recovers_prefix`, `damage_is_reported_once_not_at_every_restart`, `agent_spool_record`, `corpus_replay_agent_spool_record` | S11, S22 |
| T | The hub is told about a version that a crash then makes the agent forget, or a delta reaches the hub in pieces. | A delta becomes sendable only after one fsync at its last message (fsync batching: one per logical delta). | T | A sequence number is used twice, so an acknowledgement for one message removes another. | One counter numbers everything the agent sends, including the answers to the hub's own requests, which are never spooled. | T | A message names a file in the spool volume, or a path in a delta is used to open one. | The spool names its files itself (`seg-<hex>.lks`, `state`); no name comes from a message, a path in a delta or a file's content. | R | Versions are dropped (the spool is full, a record is damaged, a delta never finished) and nobody learns of it. | The loss is a `SpoolGap`: the time range gone, by `min` and `max` so it never runs backwards, and how many entries. | R | A write, delete or restart cannot be attributed, or its trace is lost. | The agent holds no audit trail of its own: the hub writes the audit event in the same transaction as the command (S9, 03a). | I | A secret reaches a log, an error, a `Debug` print or a metric: the ID token, the join token, the key. | Tokens and key bytes are `Secret<T>`: redacted in `Debug`, zeroized on drop, never in an error. | I | File content, or text the cluster chose, reaches a log, a metric or a reply. | Logs hold paths, hashes and ids. Metric labels come from closed enums. | I | File content rests on a second volume: whoever reads the spool reads config, and a damaged record could end up in a log. | The spool's files are created mode `0600`, on a volume of their own that the NFS root does not contain (a configuration whose spool is inside the root is refused at start), and hold exactly the delta messages: a denied entry (D79) has a name, a size and a hash and no bytes, in the spool as on the wire. | I | The health port discloses internals to whatever can reach it. | It answers `GET` and `HEAD` on `/healthz`, `/readyz` and `/metrics` only, with a few bytes that name no cause; metrics are counters with closed label sets and hold no path. | D | A hub (or a flood on the stream) sends oversized or numerous messages. | Messages are capped at 4 MiB after decompression (`proto::limits`). | D | The agent's memory or queues grow without bound. | Everything is bounded: the outbox holds 64 messages and 8 MiB; a delta is at most 3 MiB; the tree stops at 100,000 entries and 64 levels; the root ring keeps at most 512 roots, 16 MiB and one hour; file operations share 64 lock stripes; the blocking pool has 16 threads; the Kubernetes projection is capped. | D | The hub is down, flapping or unreachable, and the agent hammers it or gives up. | Reconnect delays use full-jitter exponential backoff capped at 60 seconds; a connection that does not last does not reset the backoff; HTTP/2 keepalive finds a dead path. | D | The hub's settings make the agent burn CPU or read more. | `AgentConfig` is clamped: scans every 5 to 300 seconds, heartbeats every 10 to 300 seconds, files at most 2 MiB. | D | Hostile content on the export stalls scans: huge files, deep trees, endless names, unreadable directories. | A file over 2 MiB is skipped and reported, one over 64 MiB is not hashed; depth and entry counts are capped; names that cannot be an `NfsPath` are hashed by their raw bytes and reported; a directory that cannot be read keeps its previous subtree. | D | A slow client holds the health port, or the agent hangs and nobody notices. | At most 16 connections, 5 seconds for the headers, 10 seconds per connection. `/healthz` fails when a loop has ended or the scanner makes no progress for 11 minutes, and the kubelet restarts the container. | `a_client_that_sends_nothing_is_cut_off_and_the_connection_limit_holds`, `a_loop_that_ended_fails_healthz_at_once_and_a_hub_outage_does_not`, `healthz_fails_when_the_scanner_stops_making_progress_and_only_then` | S16 |

| T | A heartbeat shows a root the hub has no delta for, so a hub asks for a delta in the middle of a copy and sees half of it. | The heartbeat carries the root the hub was last told about (baseline, delta or answer), not the one being held. | E | The agent's Kubernetes rights are used to reach more of the cluster. | Namespaced Roles only, per configured namespace, plus one Role for the two named Secrets in the agent's own namespace; no wildcard, no ClusterRole, no Secret `list`. | E | A parser flaw in untrusted input (a hub message, a PEM file, a certificate, an ID token, a path) crashes the agent or worse. | No unsafe code, no panics outside tests (clippy-denied), typed values at the boundary. ### Residual risks

- **A deny glob decides by name, not by content (D79).** `*.pem` finds a key called `server.pem` and does not find one called `id_rsa`, `credentials` or `app.yml`, nor a password inside an ordinary config file. The built-in list is the one D79 names; the hub can add globs, and the secret scan on the hub (S14b) is the second line for values inside files. The agent cannot tell a key from its bytes without reading them, which is what the list exists to avoid.
- **Bytes that were spooled before a path became denied stay on the spool volume.** When the hub adds a glob, the pump strips the bytes from every record it replays, so they never reach the connection; but a record already written to a segment file keeps them until the hub has acknowledged it and the segment is deleted (mode `0600`, on the agent's own volume). The same holds for the hub: what it received before it sent the glob it still has, and marking a file denied does not change the Merkle root (the root commits to content, not to policy), so the hub is not told again about a file that does not change. Rewriting the spool on a change of globs would close the first part; it is not built.
- **The hash of a denied file is sent (D79).** The name, the size and the SHA-256 of the content go to the hub, because the hub must see that the file exists and that it changed. A hash lets whoever holds it check a guess of the content offline; for a random key that is no help, for a short password in a file it is. A `DENIED` answer carries no hash, so the hash is not available by commanding a write or a delete either.
- **Before the hub's first configuration only the built-in globs are in force.** A glob the hub adds applies from the moment its configuration is read (before the spool is replayed, and before the next command), and a walk is started at once to re-mark the tree; files the hub's own globs deny, and the built-in globs do not, are not denied in the first moments of a start. The built-ins are never absent.
- **A hub can deny files it should not.** A glob such as `*` makes every file denied: the agent then reports names, sizes and hashes only and refuses all commands on files. That is the hub choosing to see less, and it is bounded (1,000 globs of at most 512 bytes), but it is a way for a compromised hub to make the agent useless.
- **The spool is file content at rest.** Anyone who can read the spool volume (cluster administrators, the node) reads recent config, as they can read the export. Encryption at rest is a property of the volume, not of the agent, and the files are not encrypted by it. A file that no deny glob matches, whatever it holds, is in the spool too.
- **A damaged region costs the rest of its segment.** Records are framed without a resynchronisation marker, so a bad byte in the middle of a segment loses the records after it in that segment (up to 4 MiB) and not just the one it is in. The loss is reported, and the hub's root comparison recovers the state; the intermediate versions are gone.
- **A version can be sent twice.** Delivery is at least once: after a crash, the acknowledgements of the last second may not have been written down, and a delta whose acknowledgement was in flight is sent again with the same sequence number. The hub applies a delta by hashes, so a repeat changes nothing, but a hub that records an event per delta must deduplicate by sequence number.
- **A full or failing volume loses versions, not the agent.** If the volume is smaller than `LK_SPOOL_MAX_BYTES`, or fails, an append fails (`ENOSPC`, I/O error): the delta is not spooled, the segment is abandoned, and the hub learns of the change through the heartbeat root and a `RequestDelta`. The chart must size the volume above the byte bound. Appends sit in the path of P1, so a slow volume delays every delta by its fsync.
- **A hostile writer can push history out.** Someone who can churn files on the export fills the spool faster than the hub can drain it during an outage, and the oldest whole deltas go first. The gap says so; the last state is never lost, because the tree and the heartbeat root are not in the spool.
- **A gap waits for a delta to ride on.** The loss is stamped on the next delta sent. With an empty spool and nothing changing, it waits; the hub sees the root mismatch meanwhile and asks for a full scan.
- **The hub is trusted to authorise.** A compromised hub, or an agent certificate stolen within its 24 hours, can read any regular file of at most 2 MiB under the root, write or delete one against its current hash, and restart any Deployment in the configured namespaces. The agent bounds what a command can be (typed paths, hash, size, namespace) but does not decide whether a command is wanted. The agent has no revocation list; ending a stolen identity is the hub's job (03b).
- **Key and token material cannot be wiped everywhere.** The agent wipes its own copies, but the key's bytes pass through hyper, kube, rcgen and TLS library buffers that it cannot zeroize. The Kubernetes client deserialises whole Deployments, environment values of variables that are not allowlisted included, before they are trimmed away; those strings are dropped, not wiped. The certificate Secret holds the private key in the cluster: anyone who can `get` that Secret (cluster administrators) or read the node's memory or disk can take it, and encryption at rest is a property of the cluster.
- **A join token cannot be renewed (token-only clusters).** The fallback token is single-use. If the agent loses its stream and cannot renew over it before the 24-hour certificate runs out, the re-join has no valid token, and the agent stays disconnected (`/readyz` fails) until an Admin issues a new one. Clusters with Workload Identity re-join on their own. This follows from Q26.
- **A renewed certificate reconnects the stream.** The session reconnects when the outbox is empty, or after 30 seconds at most. A command that is running at that moment gets no reply, and the hub times it out.
- **`verify` does not check the chain's signature.** `ClientIdentity::verify` checks the key, the SAN, the lifetime and the parse. The signature is checked by the TLS handshake (the hub checks our certificate against its CA on every stream), not at join.
- **NFS has no compare-and-swap.** Between the hash check and the rename, another writer on the export can change the file, and a replace or a delete then overwrites that change. The window is one rename. Creates are protected (hard link). The sentinel (17) is what shows who else wrote.
- **A file swapped for a FIFO can block a thread.** If a regular file is replaced by a FIFO between the `lstat` and the `open` (the walker and file operations both have that window), the `open` blocks until something opens the other end. The caller of a file operation gets `IO` after 30 seconds, but the blocked thread, and the lock stripe it holds, stay stuck until then; the walker's thread would stall the scan until `/healthz` fails after 11 minutes and the kubelet restarts the container. Sixteen blocking threads exist. This needs write access to the export and good timing; there is no `O_NONBLOCK` guard. This was found by reading the code and is not covered by a test.
- **Leftovers and hidden names.** A crash between staging and renaming leaves a `.lanekeeper-tmp-*` file that nothing sweeps. The walker ignores names that start with `.lanekeeper-tmp-` or `.nfs`, so anyone with write access can hide a file from the hub by using such a name. New files are mode 0644 and owned by the agent's uid; whether the config-server can read them depends on `root_squash` and the uid (Q11, 1010 by Q4), which must be settled before the agent writes on a pilot swimlane.
- **Symbolic links are invisible to the hub's commands.** A link is never followed, even inside the root, so a config file that is a link is reported as an entry the hub can neither read nor write. This is deliberate (S17).
- **A write racing a long scan.** A tool write that races a scan longer than 2 seconds may push one extra delta. It is harmless: the next walk finds the file unchanged.
- **The service-account token is mounted.** The agent needs the Kubernetes API, so anything running in the container can read the token. There is no shell and no second process, but a Role cannot limit `patch` to one annotation: a stolen token could patch the image or command of any Deployment in the configured namespaces.
- **The Kubernetes link is the client library's.** It uses rustls with aws-lc-rs and verifies the cluster CA, but the kube client also accepts TLS 1.2; only the hub link is forced to 1.3 (S6). A watcher whose list keeps failing retries with backoff for ever, logging only the HTTP status; the cluster report then stays "not synced".
- **Plain-HTTP hops.** The metadata server (node-local) and the config-server (Q37, no authentication) are reached without TLS. The NetworkPolicy contract limits who can be reached; it does not make the hop private. The config-server accepts a refresh from any pod that can reach port 8888, not only from the agent; that is a property of the endpoint, and its NetworkPolicy belongs to the chart.
- **A deny glob decides `FetchServed` by the names in the request.** The agent checks the file as named, with the application's folder, with the channel's folder and with the tenant's copy. The config-server also searches every other directory, so a file whose only denied part is a folder the request does not name (`private/app.yml` when the request is for `app.yml`) is not found by the check. Keystores are caught by their extensions wherever they sit; a hub glob that names a folder is not. The bytes of an unlisted file the server resolves are returned as the server serves them.
- **A refresh may be applied twice.** A refresh that timed out may have been applied; it is tried again (up to three attempts). A refresh is safe to repeat, so this costs a second reload at most.
- **The config-server's status is passed on as given.** A `2xx` is the hub's to read as success; the agent does not judge it. A server that answers `200` and did nothing is not noticed.
- **The start time is the oldest pod's.** `config_server_started_at` is the earliest start among the config-server Deployment's pods, so a replica that predates a folder is not hidden by a newer one. A delta that does not mention the Deployment carries none, and a Deployment with no started pod reports none, so the hub keeps its last value.
- **The log filter cannot prove a negative.** The Kubernetes client crates are silenced and other libraries are held at `warn`, but nothing proves that no other library ever logs text that the hub or the cluster chose. The agent's own logs hold file paths, which an NFS writer chooses; JSON escaping keeps them to one line.
- **The pod contract depends on the cluster.** The image build has not run (no Docker daemon where this was written), a NetworkPolicy is ignored by a CNI that does not enforce it, the metadata address and port are placeholders until a real node confirms them (Q26), the hub and API-server CIDRs are values the chart fills in (Q1), and the health port is unauthenticated. Base-image digest pins receive no fixes until someone changes them, and there is no signature or SBOM check on the image (09).
- **A hung NFS mount is noticed late.** File operations time out after 30 seconds, but `/healthz` fails only after the scanner has made no progress for 11 minutes.
- **A stale cluster delta after a reconnect.** A delta that was blocked in the 8-slot report channel while the hub was away can be sent after the full report that follows the reconnect, and then stays stale until the next change or the next report request.

- **Anyone who can create a Job in a watched namespace can open a sync window.** A Job whose name matches `LK_SYNC_JOB_NAME_GLOBS` (default `*dataload*`) or carries the configured label opens a window; changes seen while it runs are tagged with it, and the hub holds drift alerts until it closes (D75). A Job that never finishes would keep them held. The agent does not bound the length of a window: the hub (05) must, and must show a tag from a window that is older than the Job's normal run as such. Namespace write access is already trusted with the export's content (the dataload Job writes it).
- **Quiet is estimated from file times.** The quiet period counts from the newer of a changed file's mtime and ctime, read from the NFS server and compared with the agent's clock; a skew of seconds makes a batch go out early (more deltas, never a lost change) or up to that much later (P1 has about 2 s of headroom beyond the worst case). A removal has no time and counts from the walk that saw it.
- **A change hidden by attribute caching is tagged late or not at all.** If the NFS client caches attributes, a walk can miss a file written during a window until the cache expires or the 15-minute full rehash finds it; the window's close may then already have been sent, and the delta arrives untagged. The hub sees it as out-of-band drift (a false alert, not a missed one).
- **Window events are not acknowledged.** A close lost with a connection is repeated on the next one, for an hour; a hub that was away longer does not learn of it, and a window it holds open must be bounded by the hub.

## Hub identity and audit (03a)

Owner: prompt 03a. This section covers `crates/hub-identity` as built by tasks T1 to T5: Entra sign-in for the web UI (T1), users, roles and access requests (T2), sessions and CSRF (T3), default-deny authorization and bearer-token verification for MCP and agent join (T4), and the hash-chained audit log with its KMS-signed checkpoints, nightly verification and Cloud Logging copy (T5). The crate is a library; the hub binary (09) composes it, supplies the real `SecretSource`, `KmsSigner`, `Notifier`, `EventBus` and `Leases`, and runs `hub_identity::testing::assert_hub_route_table` against its full route table. The tables and the append-only trigger belong to 01 (see that section); the agent gateway, certificate issuing and the Git mirror are 03b; MCP tools are 07; the vault for GitHub tokens is 06.

The crate answers two questions. Can someone become a user, or a role, they were not given? And can what the users did be changed afterwards without anyone noticing?

### Assets

- **Sessions.** The cookie `__Host-lk_session` carries 256 random bits; the table holds only their SHA-256. Whoever holds the cookie is that user until the session ends (S2).
- **The CSRF token.** The cookie `__Host-lk_csrf` carries a second random value, and the session row holds its hash. It is what makes a state-changing request deliberate (S3).
- **Login state.** The sealed cookie `__Host-lk_login` (state, nonce, PKCE verifier, return path, 10 minutes) and the 32-byte key that seals it, held in Secret Manager and shared by the replicas (S1, S7).
- **The hub's credential to Entra.** A federated assertion (the projected service-account token, read on every exchange) or a client secret from `SecretSource` (S7, Q25).
- **Bearer tokens in transit.** Entra access tokens for MCP and Google ID tokens for agent join. Each is a credential until it expires (S1).
- **The user directory.** Roles, status and the `requires_approval` flag decide every authorization (S1, S4). The list of bootstrap admin object ids in the configuration decides who starts as Admin.
- **The trust anchors fetched at run time:** the discovery document and the signing keys (JWKS) of Entra and Google, the checkpoint public key (configuration), and the cached copies of all three.
- **The audit chain.** `audit_events` and `audit_checkpoints`, the checkpoint objects in the locked bucket, the KMS signing key's use, and the Cloud Logging copy. Together they are the evidence of who changed what (S9).
- **The chain lock.** One advisory lock serialises every audited transaction from `record` to commit, so availability of the lock is availability of every write.
- **Logs and metrics,** which must stay free of tokens, cookies, secrets and event contents (S10).

### Trust boundaries

- **Browser to hub.** Everything in a request is untrusted: cookies, headers (`Origin`, `X-CSRF-Token`, `X-Request-Id`), the query of the callback, the return path, JSON bodies. The edge parsers are strict and bounded, and a request carries no authority until the session lookup says so.
- **Hub to Entra and Google** (TLS 1.3 only, one HTTPS client with a host allowlist). Discovery documents, key sets and token responses are untrusted until they pass the checks; no URL is ever taken from a token.
- **Holders of bearer tokens** (MCP clients, agents) **to hub.** The token is checked in full before anything else is read from it.
- **Hub replicas to PostgreSQL,** as `lanekeeper_app`, which holds INSERT and SELECT only on the audit tables (01). Replicas learn of each other's role and status changes through the outbox event, not through shared memory.
- **Hub to Secret Manager and to the projected service-account token file.** The only secrets this crate reads. It has no Kubernetes client.
- **Hub to the checkpoint bucket and to KMS.** Through the GKE metadata server (plain HTTP on a link-local address, node-local) and `KmsSigner`. The hub may create objects and request signatures; it cannot overwrite or delete a checkpoint object (locked retention, create-only upload).
- **Hub to the notifier,** after the commit and with a timeout. It carries a name and a link, and a failure never rolls back the action.
- **Database owner to the audit tables.** The migrator (and anyone with the owner role) can disable the trigger. That is the boundary the checkpoints and the Cloud Logging copy exist to watch (S9).
- **This crate to the hub binary.** The crate guarantees what it registers. The binary must register every other route through the same guard and run the exported checks (S4).

### STRIDE

A row's Proof cell names tests, benchmarks or fuzz targets that exist in `crates/hub-identity`; `tests/threat_model.rs` fails if one does not. A control that is not built yet has a Proof cell that starts with `None`.

The size and shape are checked before any parsing, the signature is checked against the cached key set before any claim is read, then issuer, audience, `tid`, `exp`, `nbf` and `iat` (60 s of skew). The nonce must equal the one sealed in the login cookie (constant time). `jku`, `jwk`, `x5u` and `x5c` are never read. So that a legitimate row never differs, `at` is written by parsing the interval from text; the floating-point product it replaced was off by microseconds from the year 2255. Rows are listed one by one (the chain after a column difference is intact). A stored value that the check cannot read is a finding about its column, never an error that ends the walk: `at` is selected through a `CASE` that gives `NULL` for `infinity` and `-infinity` and as exact text otherwise, so no stored time can make the query fail (`infinity`, `-infinity` and a time past the last microsecond an `i64` holds each give `ColumnsDiffer({at})`); a `jsonb` column holding a number or a nesting `serde_json` refuses, and a `NULL` in a column that is `NOT NULL`, are reported by column name; a `NULL` in `prev_hash`, `hash` or `event_json` is a hash failure at that position. A failure of the query itself that is not about the shape of the tables (a connection, a statement or lock timeout) is a `VerifyError` (`Database` or `Timeout`), and a refusal of the shape of the tables is a `VerifyError` and also a finding (the `SchemaChanged` row below). Either ends the run, but not the findings: `verify` returns `VerifyAborted` with everything found until then, the two halves (chain, checkpoints) run whatever happens to the other, and the nightly job alerts on those findings and reports `Incomplete` (`Errored` only when there were none). Differing columns, rows forged below position 1 and every other kind of failure are listed in three separate bounded lists (100 each), so a hundred rows with a changed copy, or a hundred forged rows, never keep a broken chain or a bad checkpoint out of the report. The alert names the most severe failure first (chain, then checkpoint, then columns, then an unreadable bucket), whatever order they were found in. The actor columns are derived from the stored actor with the same function the log uses to write them (an exhaustive match), so a new kind of actor cannot make every row of that kind look tampered with; a stored actor of a kind this version does not know is reported as `actor_kind` and `actor_ref`. | `a_denormalised_column_changed_alone_is_detected_for_every_column`, `the_tamper_cases_cover_every_compared_column_changed_and_removed`, `a_rewritten_event_whose_columns_were_left_alone_is_also_a_column_failure`, `every_row_with_different_columns_is_listed_and_the_walk_goes_on_across_batches`, `columns_after_the_first_broken_link_are_not_looked_at`, `an_infinite_time_is_a_finding_and_a_later_tamper_is_still_found`, `a_negative_infinite_time_is_a_finding_and_a_later_tamper_is_still_found`, `a_time_beyond_the_microseconds_of_a_bigint_is_a_finding_and_a_later_tamper_is_still_found`, `a_jsonb_column_that_cannot_be_read_is_a_finding_and_a_later_tamper_is_still_found`, `a_row_with_a_null_in_a_chain_column_is_a_hash_failure_and_not_an_error`, `a_query_the_database_refuses_for_the_shape_of_its_tables_is_a_finding_and_a_dead_connection_is_an_error`, `a_hundred_column_failures_do_not_crowd_out_a_broken_chain_or_a_checkpoint`, `column_failures_never_crowd_out_the_other_kinds`, `every_kind_of_actor_is_a_clean_row_and_a_changed_reference_is_not`, `only_a_value_that_cannot_be_decoded_is_a_finding_and_anything_else_is_a_fault`, `a_column_difference_alerts_through_the_metric_and_notifier_and_names_columns_only`, `events_with_every_optional_field_present_verify_clean`, `events_with_every_optional_field_absent_verify_clean`, `a_mixed_chain_with_checkpoints_and_odd_timestamps_verifies_clean`, `at_is_stored_exactly_for_any_date_the_column_can_hold`, `each_column_changed_alone_is_the_only_one_reported`, `the_columns_of_any_written_event_never_differ_from_it`, `column_verifier_property_any_single_column_change_is_detected` | S9, S10 |
| T | A stored `jsonb` value makes the select itself fail or take unbounded time, so that every nightly run ends in an error and nothing after the row is ever verified. The INSERT-only role can write the two `jsonb` columns freely, and a `jsonb` number is stored compactly and printed in full: 9,000 numbers of `1e131000` are 2.5 KB stored (144 KB decompressed) and print as more than a gigabyte, which the server refuses (SQLSTATE 54000, `out of memory`, after allocating a gigabyte); 7,000 print as 917 MB and take 8 to 13 seconds each time (round 4, the reviewer's probe: one such row, then three legitimate events, ended as `Errored` after 9.4 s with nothing notified; round 3 printed the column to measure it, twice per row). Tiny exponents (`1e-16383` prints 16 KB each), a zero with the largest scale, nesting deeper than `jsonpath` reads (a hard "stack depth limit exceeded" that `silent` does not suppress) and a long run of control characters (six bytes printed for one stored) are the same class. | The select never prints a `jsonb` it has not vetted. One searched `CASE` per column, each condition evaluated only if the ones before it did not apply, and each at most once per row: (1) the stored size, from the header, over 8 KiB; (2) the size once decompressed over 8 KiB (a value stored under the limit decompresses to at most about 2 MB, and the raw size bounds the nesting to 1,024 levels, far under the depth at which `jsonpath` fails, and the number of numbers. `jsonpath` needs about 195 bytes of stack a level, so the deepest value the gate lets through needs about 200 kB of `max_stack_depth`, a tenth of the default of 2 MB: measured on Ubuntu's PostgreSQL 16.15 with the real nightly job, the verifier returns the right finding for it at 200 kB and ends `Errored` at 192 kB, and a server configured below that, which the INSERT-only role could then also fill with such a value, would be blind until the setting is raised; the consecutive-night alert is what tells the admins); (3) a number, by value and without printing it: past the range of an `i64`, or not an integer; (4) more than 16 numbers (an integer can still carry a scale that prints 16 KB); (5) only then `octet_length(col::text)` over 16 KiB, which prints at most a few hundred kilobytes. A value that is not vetted is sent as the JSON `null` literal, which no event holds in these columns, and is reported as that column differing. The bound comes from the types, not from the size of an event (an actor or the evidence of an attribution holds `ShortText` of 256 bytes and one time; the largest is about 700 bytes, and a test fails when the types grow to a tenth of the limit), which also keeps the work for a poisoned row to a few milliseconds: ten of them do not add up to a timeout. The text and hash columns are measured by `octet_length`, which reads a length from the header and cannot fail (the checkpoint columns the same). | T | An owner retypes or drops a column the verifier reads (`ALTER COLUMN hash TYPE int`, `DROP COLUMN actor_ref`, `ALTER COLUMN at TYPE text`), so that every query fails. The run is `VerifyError::SchemaChanged` and also a finding, `FailureKind::SchemaChanged`, carrying only the SQLSTATE (the server's message can quote a value and is dropped): the nightly job alerts that night as `Incomplete` ("the audit tables no longer have the shape the verifier reads (SQLSTATE 42883). The run did not finish.") and raises `audit_verify_schema_changes_total`, without waiting for a second night. Timeouts (57014, 55P03), connections (08), resources (53), operator intervention (57P) and transaction failures (40) are not class 42 and stay errors. When a retype leaves the query working, the values decide: if every value is still what the event says the log is clean (a `text` column made `varchar`), and if a value can no longer be read as the type it is read as, that column differs (`approval_id` made `int` or `numeric`, `actor_ref` made `bytea`, `at` made a `date`). | T | Two rows have the same `seq` (an owner dropped the primary key; the chain may still link, because one of the two was moved up). If a whole batch is rows at one position `>=` would ask for the same rows again, so the position is reported and the next batch starts after it: a position held by more rows than a batch (5,000 by default) is compared only as far as a batch reaches into it, and the chain then does not link after it.
