The restart itself is the ordinary one: Operator, one at a time for a service across replicas, one audit event, and a proposal for a user who needs approval. | `config_server_restart_is_offered_when_c11_is_open_and_names_affected_services`, `config_server_restart_is_not_offered_when_the_swimlane_has_no_single_config_server`, `a_config_server_restart_by_an_approval_user_is_a_proposal`, `restart_and_notify_need_operator`, `restart_allows_one_per_service_at_a_time` | S4, S9 |

### Residual risks

- **A landed write that someone else overwrites before the reconcile job looks is not audited as ours.** A write is sent to the agent after the hub keeps its intent (the hashes, the path and the audit event it will record) with the idempotency claim; if the hub then fails before it records the write, the claim stays unfinished. The retry with the same key, or `ReconcileWrites` for a claim older than `stale_claim_after` (60 s), reads the file through the agent and records the write only if the file has the hash that was sent (`resolve` in `reconcile.rs`). If somebody wrote the file in between, the hash differs and the claim is cleared: no audit event is recorded for our write, the intent holds the hash and not the bytes so the version we wrote is not kept, and whether it was ever seen depends on a registry scan having run between the two writes (05, D47). The retry then meets a conflict, not a second write. The window is the time from the landed write to the first look: nothing for a retry after a request that gave up and knew it (it marks its claim, and the retry looks at once), and for a request that died, until its claim is 60 s old and the retry or the next run of the job finds it. The file that is there afterwards is seen by the registry's next scan (05), so its content is not hidden; what is missing is the record of our write.
- **A restart whose answer is lost is not recorded, and a retry restarts again.** `RestartDeployment` cannot be checked afterwards the way a file can: there is no hash to look for. When the command was sent and no usable answer came back (a timeout, a closed stream), `send_and_record` in `ops/restart.rs` releases the claim, writes no audit event and returns `Unavailable`; the Deployment may or may not be rolling. A retry with the same key claims again and restarts again, which is harmless to a Deployment (it rolls to the same template) but means that a restart can happen with no audit event, and that two can happen for one. The hub logs a warning with the swimlane. The same holds for a restart that an admin approves: the proposal stays pending. The only other evidence is outside this crate: whatever the agent (02) logs for the command and the cluster's own audit log.
- **The GitHub token passes through buffers that cannot be zeroized.** `PUT /api/v1/me/github-token` is read by axum as `Bytes` and the handler keeps that body alive for the whole vault call, until it is freed unzeroed; the same bytes pass through hyper's read buffer, rustls's buffers (the hub speaks TLS 1.3 itself, D25) and serde_json's scratch buffer when the text has escapes. The outbound request carries the token in an `Authorization` header that octocrab builds and hyper writes into its write buffer and rustls's record buffer; the header value is marked sensitive but is `Bytes` and is not wiped. What the hub controls is wiped: the caller's `Secret<String>`, the cipher's buffer, the data key and the `Secret` that `with_token` hands out. Reading the rest needs access to the process's memory, which is outside this model (S16, and the same limit as the platform's, 03b).
- **A token save holds a user row lock and a database connection across one KMS call.** `store` in `vault/mod.rs` opens a transaction, locks the user's row with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, reserves the credential row, and only then calls KMS to wrap the data key, because the credential id that the associated data needs is known only once the row exists. It commits after the audit event. The whole transaction is inside `db_timeout` (10 s), and a timeout rolls it back, but until then it holds one pool connection and the user's row, so a slow KMS delays that user's other saves and any other writer of the user's row, and concurrent saves of one user each wait for the lock on a connection of their own. The call needs an Editor, a valid token that GitHub accepts with push access to both repositories, and the user's turn at the per-user rate limit; the audit chain lock is taken after the KMS call, not during it.
- **If a proposal stops being pending between the NFS write and its recording on approval, the write is still audited and the proposal is left as it is.** `follow::apply` locks the proposal's status in the recording transaction, and if it is no longer `pending` (two admins cannot: a lease serialises them; the expiry job cannot while the approval holds its lease, because it takes the same `proposal:{id}` lease for each proposal and skips one whose lease is held, which `the_expiry_job_skips_a_proposal_whose_approval_is_at_the_agent` shows) it logs a warning, records the audit event and the registration anyway because the file changed, and does not write the decision. The admin is told the change was applied; the proposal reads `expired` and has no approval row, and the audit event names the approver, the author and the proposal. What is left is a command that outlasts the 60-second lease: then the job can expire the proposal, and the case below is what happens. `a_proposal_that_expires_while_its_write_is_on_the_way_is_still_audited_and_left_expired` holds the behaviour.
- **A new JSON reader must go through `object`, or serde reads an array as the struct.** serde accepts `["a.yml", ...]` for a struct and `deny_unknown_fields` does not stop it. The readers of T5 were fixed first; the readers of T3 and T4 (edit, revert, restart, draft, approve, reject) had the same flaw and now read through `object` in `routes/wire.rs`, which refuses a body whose first byte after white space is not `{`, with `a_json_array_is_not_an_object_for_any_reader` as the proof. The risk that remains is a reader added later that calls serde directly; `tests/threat_model.rs` fails if `wire.rs` has a second `serde_json::from_slice`. The effect of the flaw was a body the contract does not describe being accepted, never a bypass of a check: every value was still typed and every action authorised.
- **The Teams allowlist may need the tenant's host (Q9).** The default hosts are `*.logic.azure.com`, `*.api.powerplatform.com` and `*.webhook.office.com`. A Workflows webhook whose address is on another host is logged as "not an address that may be called" and the notification is dropped, with no error to the action: the alerts, including audit-chain alerts, never reach Teams until an admin adds the host to `TeamsConfig::allowed_hosts`. The opposite also holds: the three suffixes are shared Azure infrastructure, so the allowlist keeps a card from going to an arbitrary site but not to another Logic App; the URL and the choice of destination are an admin's (a secret name in `notification_settings`), and a card holds names, ids and counts, never file content. Q9 has not named the tenant's host, and the notifier does nothing until a destination is configured.
- **Rejected and conflicting writes leave no audit event.** An action that changes nothing (a conflict, a validation rejection, a lock, a denied path) writes nothing to the chain, as the rule requires; a conflict or a rejection is stored as the answer of its idempotency claim for 24 hours and shows in the logs and the platform's request metrics. Probing for forbidden paths or repeated conflicts is therefore visible in the logs and the RED metrics and not in the tamper-evident trail.
- **One GitHub account can be linked to several users, and no route resets a link yet.** The link is `users.github_login`, and no index makes it unique, so two users who share a token are both allowed; GitHub attributes both pull requests to one account, while the audit event of each names the Lanekeeper user. `reset_github_link` exists as a method (Admin, audited) but has no route (plan A11): an admin cannot unlink through the app until one is added, and a user who changed GitHub accounts is refused until then.
- **The body limits are larger than S11's 4 MiB, and the platform's cap is what binds.** The edit and draft routes allow 12 MiB and 64 KiB of body, because a 2 MiB text can need six bytes a character in JSON; the hub's limits layer (`body_max`, 4 MiB by default) wraps the routes and the smaller limit wins, so an escape-heavy file near 2 MiB is a 413 through the web app. That is the safe direction. When 09 mounts these routes the hub's limit applies; if it raises `body_max`, the memory of one in-flight edit rises with it, bounded by the per-user and per-IP rates and the request timeout.
- **The value-based scan is heuristic, and with the deny globs it is the barrier before content leaves for GitHub.** A secret in a shape the engine does not know (its section lists the limits and Q20) is saved to NFS and committed to the pull request. Both repositories belong to the organisation, and a flagged value is never shown, but nothing stops a secret that the scan does not recognise.
- **A job uses its author's token in the background, after the request that asked for it.** S8 and D42 say a token is used only inside its owner's own authenticated request; a pull request is made by a job (`pr_jobs`) that may run minutes later and on another replica, and the scan resumes one after a crash. This was a deliberate reading of D77 ("PR creation runs as a persisted background job with states"), and **a human DECIDED to allow it on 2026-10-15: D107** (a PR job may use its author's GitHub token at job time; it relaxes S8 and D42 for PR jobs only, and every other use of the token stays inside the owner's own request). D107 is recorded on branch `claude/brave-cori-2cnesi` (commit `564948a`) and lands with main; until main is merged into this branch it is not in this branch's `docs/decisions.md`. The controls that stay, and that the code has: every step asks the directory again for the user (`require_active`, then `authorize` for Editor or above), a token past its expiry is refused, there is one lease per job, tries are bounded (5), a step is limited to 60 s, and the token is never logged, audited or returned. The job opens only its author's credential, so a disabled or demoted author's job fails with no call to GitHub. **The residual risk stays:** the code does not check that a session for the author still exists. A user who has signed out, or whose session was revoked, while still an active Editor, has the job run to the end; demoting or disabling the user is what stops it, and signing out is not. The alternative (the job waits for the person to be present) would stop a pull request from surviving a restart, which D77 asks for.
- **`POST /drafts` takes an `Idempotency-Key` and does not use it, so a retry makes a second draft.** `propose_draft` in `drafts.rs` authorizes the caller and inserts a row; it makes no claim. Honouring the key needs a stored response that is a draft (a new kind beside an outcome and a job), a claim replay for it, and the claim finished in the transaction that inserts the draft; that is a change to the shared idempotency store, left for a human to weigh. The harm is bounded: a draft changes nothing and writes no audit event, a user has at most 100 open drafts (`MAX_OPEN_DRAFTS`), and a draft expires after a day. `a_retried_draft_with_the_same_key_makes_a_second_draft_within_the_open_limit` holds the behaviour.
- **A command that timed out at the hub can still run at the agent, later.** `WriteFile` and `DeleteFile` carry no deadline on the wire (`proto/agent.proto`, a contract), and the gateway's timeout only ends the hub's wait; a command already written to the agent's stream is not taken back. If the agent was slow (a stalled NFS mount is the likely cause), it can run the command after the hub has answered `Unavailable`, after the reconcile job looked at the file and cleared the claim, and so with no audit event. The expected hash bounds it: the agent writes only if the file is still the one the request saw, and the change is then found by the next scan as an NFS change that no hub request made. The look at the file that settles a claim after a timeout is not itself early: it is a `ReadFile` to the same agent, and the agent's `FileOps::read` takes the same per-path lock as `write` (`blocking` in `crates/agent/src/fileops.rs`) and the lock is held until the blocking work has really finished, so a read-back queues behind a write that has begun. If the lock is not free within the agent's 30 s the read-back answers `IO`, which `resolve` takes for "cannot be asked" and leaves the claim as it is. What is left is a command that has not taken the lock when the read does. The agent runs each command as its own task, so the order in which two commands take the lock is not guaranteed, but after a timeout the read-back is sent at least one agent timeout after the write, so a write that is still waiting for the lock by then is waiting behind another operation on that path, and the read-back queues behind it as well. Fixing it needs a deadline in the command, which is a contract change for a human to approve.
- **A pull request that cannot be recorded is on GitHub with no link.** The job opens the pull request, and only then records its links and its audit event in one transaction. The checks for a second pull request for the same change (the request, the insert under a lock, the plan, and for an approval a refusal while any unfinished job of the swimlane covers one of the files) leave one gap: two jobs for the same change that reach `opening` at the same moment, where the second `pr_links_one_open` violation fails the job after its pull request is open. The job's sentence names the number and the pull request stays on GitHub with no link and no audit event of ours, for a person to close. A store that does not answer while the pull request is being recorded is not counted as a try, on purpose: failing the job would leave the pull request with no record at all, so the scan takes the job up again every 30 s until it answers, and until then the job holds one of the scan's 50 places. A store that does not answer at any other step (the blobs of the plan, the user, the lease) is counted like GitHub not answering, and the job fails after 5 tries (`a_blob_store_that_refuses_is_counted_and_the_job_fails_when_the_tries_are_used`); a database that is down cannot even write the count, and the job waits.
- **Raising a pull request leaves no audit event until it exists.** Accepting a job writes none (it changes only the job's own bookkeeping), and a job that fails writes none; the failure is in `pr_jobs.failure` and the log. Probing for pull requests that fail (a path with a secret in it, a branch that is taken) is visible to an operator in those and in the request metrics, and not in the audit chain.
- **The job's reads can be a scan behind NFS, and a failed approval leaves the proposal `approved`.** The hash a link records is the hash of the bytes the plan read through the registry, which is the pull request's content, and the registry may lag NFS by a scan; the next scan of the file shows the drift. An approved proposal becomes `applied` only when the job has opened the pull request; one whose job fails stays `approved`, and the author raises the pull request again, as a new proposal. The approval's re-check reads the registry too, not the agent (a read of each file from the agent would move up to 100 files of 2 MiB for one click), so a file that NFS has changed and the registry has not yet seen passes it. What the job then commits is the registry's version, which is the version the admin approved: the job reads each file again when it runs, compares its hash with the one the proposal holds, and fails with `a file changed after the proposal was approved` when they differ, so a pull request never carries a version nobody looked at. The pull request can be a scan older than NFS, and the next scan shows the drift.
- **`raise_pr` through `WriteService` cannot say why a job failed or give the job to its caller.** `WriteOutcome` has no variant for an accepted job or for a reason, so the trait waits up to 30 s for the job and answers `Applied`, `Rejected` (every failure is `NotAllowed`) or `Unavailable` (the job goes on, and a retry with the same key finds it); the REST route answers 202 with the job and carries the sentence. A user who requires approval gets the proposal outcome from `POST /prs` (200), which the `createPr` operation does not list. Both want a contract change before prompt 07 uses `raise_pr` for the MCP tool.
- **The reveal's limit is kept by each replica, in memory.** `RevealLimiter` is a bounded table of per-user windows (a mutex-guarded map, not a cache), so with several replicas a user can reveal `reveals_per_minute` times on each in the same minute, and a restart empties it. The limit slows a sweep for secrets and bounds the audit chain's growth; it is not the control that decides whether a reveal is allowed (the role is), and every reveal is recorded wherever it happens. A table of 10,000 users never evicts a window that still limits its user: when it is full, windows a whole window old are swept out, and a newcomer is refused with a wait (fail closed) while every window is live. So the limit cannot be reset by crowding the table; the price is that, with 10,000 users revealing inside one minute on one replica, the next new user is told to wait, and accounts are Entra's.
- **A reveal needs no approval and no second person.** D79 allows an Editor and above, and a user who `requires_approval` can reveal too, because a reveal changes nothing. The controls are after the fact: the record with the reason, the file's hash, the setting and the line, the rate limit, and the audit chain's review. Someone who is allowed to see a secret can write it down; nothing in the hub prevents that.
- **A reveal is not replayable, so a response that is lost is audited twice.** The route reads the `Idempotency-Key` header as the contract says (a missing or malformed one is a 400) but does not claim it: a stored answer would be the secret. A client that retries after a lost response makes a second reveal and a second event, which is the truth, and both count against the limit.
- **What can be revealed is what the mask hides, and the mask is only as good as the scan.** A secret in a shape the scan does not recognise is not masked anywhere and is not a reveal. A file that cannot be scanned (too many matches, or the scan refuses it) is masked whole, so every setting in it can be revealed, one audited call each. The setting path the person names is recorded only after the file has been shown to hold it.
- **The revealed value passes through buffers that cannot be zeroized.** `Secret` keeps the value out of `Debug`, logs and errors and wipes the copy the desk holds, but the bytes of the file the registry returned, the text the engine parsed and its settings, the serialised body (`serde_json::to_vec`) and what axum and hyper buffer for the response are plain `Bytes` and `String`s, not wiped when freed. The value lives in the process for the length of the request and until the allocator reuses the memory, as the token's buffers do (above).
- **Notify and the config-server restart are built, and stay off the real system until Q37 and Q10 are answered.** `/update-resources` has no authentication (Q37, no default): the chart's NetworkPolicy is off by default, so the hub's Operator rule and the audit trail are the only controls until the config-server's owners allow the agent and nobody else, and approve the call. Q10 (whether ArgoCD, with self-heal on, accepts the `restartedAt` annotation of a restart or rolls it back) has not been tested, and it covers the restart of the config-server as it does any other. Q35 (do services subscribe to change notifications) has no answer either: the hub reads `CONFIG_CLIENT_MONITOR_ACTIVEMQ_ENABLED` from each Deployment (D88) and believes it. Nothing in the code needs the answers; going live does.
- **A notification whose answer is lost is not recorded, and a retry notifies again.** `NotifyConfigServer` is sent with no connection held. When no usable answer comes back (a timeout, a closed stream), `execute` in `ops/notify.rs` releases the claim, records no audit event and returns `Unavailable`: the config-server may have refreshed its clients. A retry with the same key notifies again, which is harmless (a notification refreshes a cache), but the first call stays unaudited, and a lost answer for an automatic notification is logged and not retried at all. The config-server's own log and the agent's are the only record.
- **The config-server's `2xx` is taken at its word, and the pickup states are the engine's reading of it.** The agent passes the status on and does not judge it (02), so a server that answers `200` and does nothing makes services look live. `last_notified_at` is the swimlane's, not a path's, and the engine's `pickup_state` takes one such time for a service: if 05 recomputes from it alone, a change in a folder the notification did not name looks told. This module is stricter (only services that read a notified path are updated), so the two can disagree until 05 reads the services' own state. The hub reads `CONFIG_CLIENT_CACHE_TTL` with its own small parser (seconds, `ms`, `s`, `m`, `h`, `d`, ISO), which 05 may read differently. A service whose waiting changes the hub cannot tell apart (a new folder looks like any other file by its path) is left as it is when it waits for the config-server.
- **A Deployment that declares no `SPRING_APPLICATION_NAME` is assumed to be the application of the folder that has its name.** D88 gives the hub only the environment values the agent reports, so an application whose name is set in its packaged `application.yml` (or anywhere but the Deployment's environment) is not known by that name. Spring Boot's own default is not the Deployment's name, so this is a convention (the folders of the fixtures are named like their Deployments), not a fact. `ops/pickup.rs` applies it in three places: a folder is an application's when a Deployment of the swimlane is called that (`deployment_names`), `<name>.yml` in it is a property source for that Deployment (`undeclared_name`), and the statement that picks the services already matched by name. If a Deployment's real name is another, a notification for its folder is read as a search location for every service, which updates the pickup state of services that did not read the file, and a file named like the Deployment is read as a resource, which can show `live_within_ttl` for a change that needs a restart. The registry's own recomputation (05) may read the same Deployments differently until the two share one definition of an application's name; a human confirms the convention (or the agent reports the name the client uses) before this is relied on.
- **Auto-notify is best effort, and its queue is in memory.** The queue (`auto_notify_queue`, 1,024 paths) is lost when the process stops, a path that finds it full is dropped and counted (`auto_notify_dropped`), a call that fails is logged and not retried, and a replica announces only the writes it handled. The switch (`swimlanes.auto_notify`) is read inside the transaction that records the write, so it is the switch the write committed under and turning it on does not announce earlier writes, not even one whose answer is still on its way. The person can always press the button, which is why nothing here retries; the cost is a service that waits for a notification nobody sent until someone notices on the pending-restarts screen. A stop of the batcher waits for the calls under way and drops the paths still in its window.
- **The config-server is found by its Deployment name.** The setting config_server_deployment (default `csp-configuration-server`) is matched against the cluster report's Deployments of the swimlane; with none, or with several of that name in several namespaces, no restart is offered, and a swimlane whose config-server has another name offers none until the setting is changed. The affected services are every other service of the swimlane, which over-names them (a service that never asks the config-server is listed) and is cut at 2,000; when it is cut the offer says so (`truncated`, as the restart-all offer does: `a_config_server_offer_that_leaves_services_out_says_so`), and a screen that ignores the flag shows a partial list as the whole.
- **An approver sees only the first path of a notification, and no action.** `ProposalView` has one `path` and no `action` (the T4 report says so), so a proposal to notify the config-server of ten paths is listed with the first one, a zero content hash and no hunks. The audit event of the approved notification names every path, and the approver is an admin who may read the proposal's row, but the queue does not show what is about to be sent. A change to the view is a contract change.
- **The notify route's answer for an approval user is not in the contract.** `api/openapi.yaml` gives `POST /swimlanes/{id}/notify` one `200` answer, `NotifyResult`, though a user who needs approval gets a proposal (as prompt 06 requires and migration 0004 allows). The route answers that user with the `proposal_created` body every other write route uses; a generated client that validates the `200` against `NotifyResult` alone would reject it. It needs a contract change (a `oneOf` as `WriteResult` has), which this crate may not make.
- **A proposal that is deleted elsewhere leaves its `blob_refs` row, and the blob it keeps, behind.** `proposals/store.rs` keeps the version a proposal proposes with a `blob_refs` row (`ref_kind = 'proposal'`, `ref_id` the proposal's number), and `ref_id` is `text` with no foreign key to `proposals`, so the database does not remove the row when the proposal goes. This crate never deletes a proposal: it only moves `status` (`expired`, `rejected`, `stale`, `applied`), and D78 keeps every version a proposal references, so the row is meant to outlive the decision. A proposal does disappear when its swimlane is deleted (`proposals.swimlane_id` is `ON DELETE CASCADE`, and a swimlane with a decided proposal cannot be deleted at all, migration 0004), which is the registry's door and not this crate's; the rows it leaves keep their blobs out of the garbage collector's reach until the collector treats a `proposal` reference to a proposal that no longer exists as unreferenced. The fix is outside this prompt: a migration (a trigger on `proposals` that deletes its `blob_refs`, or a collector that joins `ref_id` to `proposals.id`), so it is a contract follow-up for 01 and 03b. The cost is disk held for the life of a deleted swimlane's undecided proposals, not a disclosure: the blob was already visible to the people who could see the proposal.
- **A delete found gone by the reconcile step may not have been this request's.** A delete keeps its intent with the claim before the command goes to the agent, and the reconcile step (the retry of the same key, or the `ReconcileWrites` job) records the delete as done when `ReadFile` says the file is not there. Another change can remove the file between the intent and the command, or the hub can die before it sends anything and someone else delete the file, and the agent keeps no receipt of a `DeleteFile` that a read could ask for (the wire has none, and `proto/agent.proto` is a contract), so the absence cannot be told apart. The event is still recorded, because the file is gone and a delete that is not recorded at all is the worse record, but its `diff` ends with a note that the file was found gone and that it cannot be told whether this request's delete or another change removed it, so nobody reads a `file_deleted` of that kind as proof of who deleted. A command that carries a request id the agent remembers for a while would close it; that is a contract follow-up for 02.

## MCP server (07)

Owner: prompt 07. This section covers `crates/hub-mcp` as built by tasks T0 to T7: the compatibility stub and the three deployment switches (T0), authentication in both modes, the personal-token store and its REST routes, and the gate in front of `/mcp` (T1), the 27 tools of `docs/mcp-tools.md` with their output pipeline, limits and sessions (T2), the confirmation of writes (T3), the plugin package and the administrator's guide (T4), redaction and context (T6): the final sweep over every result, the denied-file floor in every tool that takes or returns a file, the attribution and severity that go with a change, and the matrices and the fuzz target that prove them, and the served view and the notify tool (T7): `get_served_config`, and `notify_services`, which ships switched off. It was written from the code and its tests, not from the plan's intent. Where the code and plan 07 differ, the code wins and the difference is stated; the two differences a human accepted for now are the first two residual risks (D-1 and D-2, recorded as D109 in `docs/decisions.md`). A control that is not built yet is marked `None (T6)` or `None (T7)` in the table, and `tests/threat_model.rs` fails when such a marker and the code disagree; with T7 built, none is open.

The server is an Axum route group in the hub: rmcp's Streamable HTTP at `/mcp`, behind a gate. A caller is a person's Cursor, or a script holding a personal token. The server computes no fact: tools call `RegistryRead`, `DocSearch` and `WriteService` with the caller's `User`, and the engine's secret scan decides what is hidden. So the questions are: can someone who is not an active user, or not the user they claim, get a result or a write; can hostile text that the assistant reads make it write something the person did not mean; can a secret in config or docs leave in a result; and can one caller take the server from the others.

A row's Proof cell names tests, benchmarks or fuzz targets; `tests/threat_model.rs` fails if one does not exist or is a helper or an ignored test. A name written `hub-identity:name`, `hub:name` or `xtask:name` is a test of that crate. A proof marked (pg) runs against a real Postgres 16 and prints `SKIPPED` without one unless `LK_REQUIRE_DOCKER=1` is set, as `just verify-07` sets it; a skipped run proves nothing. A Proof cell that starts with `None` says that no control exists, or that the control is not built yet, and why.

### Assets

- **The authority to change things.** Six tools act on the world (`apply_change`, `upload_file`, `delete_file`, `raise_pr`, `restart_service` and `notify_services`, which ships switched off because the config-server's update endpoint has no authentication, Q37); `propose_change` records a draft and writes nothing to NFS. A wrong write reaches NFS, Git or a running service of a bank (S14, S4).
- **The person's consent.** The answer to the elicitation question is the one thing between an injected instruction and a write. Its integrity includes what the question says and whether the person can read it.
- **Bearer credentials.** Entra access tokens in transit (OAuth mode) and personal tokens (personal-token mode): `lkpat_` and 256 random bits, kept by a person in an environment variable for up to 7 days, and in the database only as an HMAC under the key `mcp-token-hmac-key` from Secret Manager (S1, S7, S14).
- **The `mcp_tokens` table** (migrations 0005 and 0006): owner, label, HMAC, expiry, revocation.
- **Config and doc content returned by tools.** Files are meant to hold no secrets (D18) but can (keystores, a pasted token, a password in a value), and everything a tool returns can reach Cursor's model providers (S14b, S15).
- **Sessions and open questions.** The MCP session ids, the table that remembers who opened each, a question waiting for an answer, the memory of finished writes, the rate buckets and the caches. They live in one replica's memory (A5).
- **The tool contract.** `src/schemas/tools.json`, which a test holds equal to `docs/mcp-tools.md`: each tool's role, confirmation and size cap. The roles equal the REST roles (the xtask lint).
- **The read path, shared with the web UI.** Twenty read tools within the P9 budget, 64 MiB of file bytes in flight, 96 MiB of caches (P11).
- **The audit evidence.** The event of every write made through MCP (written by 06, with `via=mcp`) and of every personal-token change (written here, S9).
- **Logs and metrics.** They must hold no token, no argument, no file text and no hash of a token (S10).

### Trust boundaries

- **Cursor to `/mcp`.** Everything in a request is untrusted: headers, the session id, the JSON-RPC body, the tool arguments and the answers to a question. The credential is a bearer header only, never a cookie, so a page in a browser has nothing to forge. The path to the hub is the users' HTTPS load balancer, which admits only the VPN ranges (D25, S18).
- **The assistant to the tool arguments.** The model reads text that others wrote, so what it asks for is untrusted: arguments are parsed into typed values, and text it supplies is never trusted in the question the person reads.
- **File and doc text to the model.** Tools return text from NFS, Git and docs. The only boundaries are the data labels, the redaction and the confirmation on writes (S15).
- **The hub to Cursor's model providers.** Outside our control: everything a tool returns can reach them (see Known residual risks, below).
- **The gate to rmcp.** rmcp 3.5.0 is a third-party SDK behind the gate. It sees only requests that passed the Host, Origin, token and session-owner checks; its session manager is wrapped with a cap, and its log output is held at `warn` by the hub's filter.
- **This crate to the ports** (`TokenVerifier`, `Users`, `RegistryRead`, `DocSearch`, `WriteService`). Each call carries the caller's `User`, and each write a `WriteCtx` with `Via::Mcp`, the idempotency key and the request id. What a caller may see, what is written and the audit event are decided behind the port. This crate trusts the ports and cannot check them.
- **This crate to the engine.** The secret scan, the text decoder and the settings index run in `spawn_blocking`; hostile content is the engine's section.
- **The hub to Postgres and Secret Manager.** Personal tokens are read and written as `lanekeeper_app` (SELECT and INSERT, and UPDATE of `revoked_at` and `last_used_at` only). The HMAC key is read once at start.
- **A browser to the token routes** (`/api/v1/me/mcp-tokens`). A signed-in session (cookie, Viewer) and an `Origin` check; the double-submit half of S3 is not there (D-2, D109).
- **Replicas to each other.** No shared MCP state. A revoked personal token reaches another replica within the lookup cache time, 5 seconds.
- **Cursor's approval prompt and tool allowlist.** Set by an administrator in Cursor, outside the server. `docs/cursor-admin.md` generates the allowlist from the contract (20 read tools, no write tool).
- **The hub binary to this crate.** The crate guarantees what it registers. The binary must compose the verifier, users, ports and key source into `McpState`; it does not yet.

### STRIDE

| | Threat | Mitigation | Proof | S# |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S | A caller without a valid credential reaches `/mcp`, or presents one that is not good: no header, two `Authorization` headers, another scheme, a forged, expired, foreign-tenant or wrong-audience Entra token, one without the `mcp.access` scope, a personal token in OAuth mode, a JWT in personal-token mode. | The gate runs in front of rmcp on every method (POST, GET, DELETE) and refuses before any handler runs. It reads exactly one `Authorization: Bearer` header of at most 16 KiB (strict RFC 6750 alphabet, a slice of the header, no copy), then asks `TokenVerifier::entra_access_token` (OAuth mode) or the personal-token lookup (personal-token mode); a token of the other kind is refused as not valid without being sent anywhere. The verifier is hub-identity's and checks signature, issuer, audience, `tid` and the scope; this crate maps its verdicts onto 401 with the `WWW-Authenticate` challenge, 403 and 503 (a provider that cannot answer is never a 401) and adds `Cache-Control: no-store`. In this crate the verifier is scripted, so the audience and scope tests prove the mapping and that no handler runs; the check itself is proved in hub-identity. | `missing_header_is_401_with_resource_metadata_challenge`, `invalid_token_is_401`, `expired_token_is_401`, `wrong_audience_is_401`, `token_without_mcp_scope_is_401`, `unknown_user_is_401`, `unavailable_verifier_is_503_not_401`, `two_credentials_are_refused_and_neither_is_used`, `another_scheme_is_refused`, `token_longer_than_max_is_refused_before_parsing`, `a_personal_token_in_oauth_mode_is_401_and_never_reaches_the_verifier`, `pat_in_oauth_mode_and_jwt_in_pat_mode_are_both_401`, `every_method_of_the_endpoint_is_gated`, `a_request_the_gate_refuses_never_reaches_a_handler`, `arbitrary_bytes_never_panic_and_an_accepted_token_is_a_slice_of_the_header`, fuzz `mcp_bearer_header`, `hub-identity:entra_access_token_rejects_wrong_audience`, `hub-identity:entra_access_token_rejects_missing_mcp_scope`, `hub-identity:entra_access_token_rejects_wrong_tid`, `hub-identity:entra_access_token_rejects_forged_signature` | S1, S14, S11 |
| S | A session id is used by someone other than the user who opened it: guessed, taken from a log, replayed, or sent to a replica that did not open it. | A session id is never an authenticator. The gate binds each `Mcp-Session-Id` to the user who opened it, for POST, GET and DELETE alike (rmcp 3.5.0 never shows a GET or DELETE to a handler), and answers another user, an id it did not see opened and an id it cannot parse with a 404, as rmcp answers an unknown id, which does not say whether the id exists. The handler binds the session to its first caller as a second line. It is fail closed: a session whose owner is not remembered (an expired entry, another replica) is not served. | `session_id_is_bound_to_the_user`, `a_session_whose_owner_is_not_remembered_is_not_served`, `an_unknown_or_malformed_session_id_is_404`, `a19_request_parts_reach_the_handler_and_bind_the_session_to_one_caller`, `a19_binding_must_hold_for_get_and_delete_too_because_they_never_reach_the_handler` | S14 |
| S | A page in a browser drives `/mcp` or the token routes with a signed-in person's browser: a cross-site request, or DNS rebinding to the hub's name. | `/mcp` takes a bearer header and no cookie, so a page has no ambient credential to forge. As defence in depth the `Host` must be the hub's public origin (so a rebound name fails), an `Origin`, when one is sent, must be the public origin or one of at most 8 configured origins (a desktop client may send none; T0 records `O.origin_header` for Cursor), and a method other than POST, GET or DELETE is 405. rmcp repeats the Host and Origin check as a second line. | S | A personal token is stolen, guessed, or kept after it should have ended. | A token is `lkpat_` and 256 random bits from the CSPRNG, shown once in the response that creates it and never listed (`Cache-Control: no-store`). The database holds only `HMAC-SHA-256(key, token)` under a key from Secret Manager that the database never sees, found by an indexed unique lookup and compared again in constant time. A token lives 7 days (the schema allows at most 31), the limit is 5 live tokens per user, and revocation is final (a trigger refuses to clear it). Every call checks expiry and revocation, then the user's status. A lookup that found a live token is remembered for 5 seconds, so a revoke is immediate on the replica that served it and within 5 seconds on the others; an unknown, expired or revoked token is never remembered. | `pat_roundtrip_creates_and_authenticates`, `pat_is_stored_as_hmac_not_plaintext`, `pat_is_stored_as_hmac_not_plaintext_in_the_table`, `wrong_key_does_not_authenticate`, `expired_pat_is_401`, `a_cached_token_is_still_checked_for_expiry`, `revoked_pat_is_401_at_once_on_the_same_replica`, `revoked_pat_is_401_within_the_cache_ttl_on_another_replica`, `a_token_that_is_not_known_is_never_cached`, `at_most_five_live_tokens_per_user`, `twelve_creations_at_once_make_exactly_five`, `token_is_shown_once_and_never_listed`, `creating_a_token_shows_it_once`, `a_generated_token_has_the_documented_shape_and_parses`, `generated_tokens_do_not_repeat`, `equal_hashes_compare_equal_and_one_flipped_bit_does_not`, fuzz `mcp_pat_token`, `xtask:mcp_tokens_enforce_their_constraints`, `xtask:mcp_tokens_created_at_is_held_to_the_insert` | S7, S14 |
| S | An answer to a confirmation question that is not the person's: from another session, a second answer to the same question, or an answer after the user was disabled. | The question is a request of the session it was asked in, and its answer is a POST that passes the gate like any other (a valid bearer of the session's owner, an active user). | T | A prompt injection reaches a write: text in a file, a doc, a diff or a search hit (or a hostile PR title or note) makes the assistant call `apply_change`, `upload_file`, `delete_file`, `raise_pr`, `restart_service` or `notify_services`, which the person did not mean. | Contained on the server, not by the model. The write carries the expected hash (an upload or delete also the preview token; an apply, the draft's hash), so a file that changed is a conflict and nothing is written. | I | A secret in config or docs leaves in a tool result (S14b). | Every field of an output type that can hold file or doc text is a `RedactedText`, whose only constructor runs the engine's value-based `secret_scan` first; a source scan holds that nothing else builds one and that the `text` and `snippet` fields of the output types in `dto.rs` have that type. A doc's title and headings are doc text and take the same path. Each flagged value becomes `[REDACTED: N chars, rule]`, the engine's own marker. A file is scanned as a whole (cached by a SHA-256 of its bytes computed here, never the port's hash) and the matches are mapped onto the lines returned, so a private key whose header is above the window is still hidden; a value is redacted before it is cut to length. If the scan cannot finish (a text too large, too many matches) the text is not shown and the call is `unprocessable` (fail closed). No tool has an input that asks for a flagged value, by name or by description; revealing is an audited action of the web UI (D79). **The final sweep** (`output/sweep.rs`) is the net under all of it: on every result, whatever built it, it scans every string of `data` and the summary once more with the same scan and replaces what is left, builds `content` again from the corrected data with the code that built it (a scan of the JSON text can miss a key that sits beside an escape), scans `content` as well, counts the result in `mcp_redaction_late_total` and logs the tool and the number of values and never a value, and fails closed: a result it cannot scan is not sent. The cursor and the link are the server's and are not swept. A value found by the sweep is a defect, so the matrix below asserts that it counts nothing for every honest tool. Planted fake keys, built at run time from parts (A18), are asserted absent, and the marker present, from every tool that can carry file or doc text (15 cases over 13 tools, among them the title and the headings of a doc, a line diff and the draft of `propose_change`), in both content modes; a completeness test fails when a tool is in neither the matrix nor the list of tools that carry no text.
